Well, I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that my concentration’s gotten… unreliable. I’m a dentist, so I’m used to being precise, reading charts, keeping track of details. I paint watercolors—nothing professional, just something I picked up years ago—and I’ll be laying out a wash, trying to focus on the color or the paper, and then my mind just runs off to everything that could go wrong. Mostly, yes, but it doesn’t stay neatly in that box. Retirement is sort of the doorway into it, if that makes sense.

I worry about whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ve missed some detail in the transition. So yes, retirement is a big part of it, but my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and even I can tell the size of the worry doesn’t always match the actual problem.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, it has. More than I like to admit, probably.

At the clinic, I’ve had to slow down parts of the retirement handoff because I keep second-guessing decisions—whether I explained something clearly enough, whether I picked the right time to pass something over, whether some tiny detail is going to become a problem later. I’ll mean to handle one task and then lose half the morning going back over it.

And at home it’s not much better. I used to paint to relax—watercolors, mostly, though I’m not especially good—and gardening was sort of my steady thing. Now even those feel harder to manage, like I can’t just settle into them the way I used to. So yes, it’s definitely getting in the way of daily life.

Yes, it’s not just the retirement paperwork, no. My mind sort of… circles around all sorts of things.

I worry whether my old patients are being looked after properly, even though I know they’re capable hands. And then it turns into, you know, “What am I going to be once I’m not Dr. So-and-so at the clinic every day?” I’ll be rinsing out a watercolor brush or sorting mail and suddenly I’m thinking I’ll become useless, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo yes, retirement is a big part of it, but my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and even I can tell the size of the worry doesn’t always match the actual problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this worried on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely longer than six months. It’s been going on since I started the formal retirement process, which was about a year and three months ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the paperwork—forms, signatures, phone calls, all that business. I’ve never liked loose ends, even in the office I’d keep little notes everywhere. But then the worrying just sort of stayed with me, day after day, long after any one piece of it was handled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel a bit low, but then I’ll get distracted by something ridiculous like an old box of dental molds in the garage and spend an hour wondering why I kept them.\n\nSo… down, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d put that word on it. I’m more unsettled than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. More than I like to admit, probably.\n\nAt the clinic, I’ve had to slow down parts of the retirement handoff because I keep second-guessing decisions—whether I explained something clearly enough, whether I picked the right time to pass something over, whether some tiny detail is going to become a problem later. I’ll mean to handle one task and then lose half the morning going back over it.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. I used to paint to relax—watercolors, mostly, though I’m not especially good—and gardening was sort of my steady thing. Now even those feel harder to manage, like I can’t just settle into them the way I used to. So yes, it’s definitely getting in the way of daily life.\n\nDoctor: Aside from retirement, do you also find yourself worrying a lot about other areas of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not just the retirement paperwork, no. My mind sort of… circles around all sorts of things.\n\nI worry whether my old patients are being looked after properly, even though I know they’re capable hands. Then I’ll jump to whether the practice handoff is financially sound, or whether I missed something in the numbers. I was never really a “numbers man,” though dentistry has plenty of numbers—insurance codes, lab fees, all that nonsense.\n\nAnd then there’s this other thought that keeps coming up, which I don’t much like saying out loud: I worry I’ll become useless once I’m fully retired. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that my concentration’s gotten… unreliable. I’m a dentist, so I’m used to being precise, reading charts, keeping track of details. But lately I’ll be looking over a patient chart at the clinic and suddenly realize I’ve read the same line three times and couldn’t tell you what it said.\n\nIt happens outside work too. I paint watercolors—nothing professional, just something I picked up years ago—and I’ll be laying out a wash, trying to focus on the color or the paper, and then my mind just runs off to everything that could go wrong. And then I’m standing there with the brush in my hand thinking, “Where was I?” It’s frustrating. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things, in a way that feels hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I try to talk myself through it—almost like I would with a nervous patient in the chair, you know, calmly laying out what’s actually happening versus what I’m imagining. But it doesn’t really stop there.\n\nOne concern just sort of hands off to the next. I’ll think, “Did I answer that email clearly?” and then I’m checking it again, and then I’m reviewing notes, and then maybe asking someone the same question I already asked because I can’t quite let it rest. It feels like I’m trying to be careful, but it gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems start after something particularly stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it one big stressful event, exactly. It’s more that it started around when I began the formal retirement process—about a year and three months ago now.\n\nWhich is funny, because people keep saying retirement is supposed to be relaxing. I had this idea I’d spend more time painting, maybe organize the garage, finally sort through all these old dental journals I’ve kept for no good reason. But once the paperwork and planning started, that’s when I noticed all this really beginning.\n\nDoctor: Are most of your worries mainly about retirement?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, but it doesn’t stay neatly in that box. Retirement is sort of the doorway into it, if that makes sense.\n\nI worry about whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ve missed some detail in the transition. And then it turns into, you know, “What am I going to be once I’m not Dr. So-and-so at the clinic every day?” I’ll be rinsing out a watercolor brush or sorting mail and suddenly I’m thinking I’ll become useless, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo yes, retirement is a big part of it, but my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and even I can tell the size of the worry doesn’t always match the actual problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this worried on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely longer than six months. It’s been going on since I started the formal retirement process, which was about a year and three months ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the paperwork—forms, signatures, phone calls, all that business. I’ve never liked loose ends, even in the office I’d keep little notes everywhere. But then the worrying just sort of stayed with me, day after day, long after any one piece of it was handled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel a bit low, but then I’ll get distracted by something ridiculous like an old box of dental molds in the garage and spend an hour wondering why I kept them.\n\nSo… down, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d put that word on it. I’m more unsettled than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. More than I like to admit, probably.\n\nAt the clinic, I’ve had to slow down parts of the retirement handoff because I keep second-guessing decisions—whether I explained something clearly enough, whether I picked the right time to pass something over, whether some tiny detail is going to become a problem later. I’ll mean to handle one task and then lose half the morning going back over it.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. I used to paint to relax—watercolors, mostly, though I’m not especially good—and gardening was sort of my steady thing. Now even those feel harder to manage, like I can’t just settle into them the way I used to. So yes, it’s definitely getting in the way of daily life.\n\nDoctor: Aside from retirement, do you also find yourself worrying a lot about other areas of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not just the retirement paperwork, no. My mind sort of… circles around all sorts of things.\n\nI worry whether my old patients are being looked after properly, even though I know they’re capable hands. Then I’ll jump to whether the practice handoff is financially sound, or whether I missed something in the numbers. I was never really a “numbers man,” though dentistry has plenty of numbers—insurance codes, lab fees, all that nonsense.\n\nAnd then there’s this other thought that keeps coming up, which I don’t much like saying out loud: I worry I’ll become useless once I’m fully retired. Like once the office is gone, what exactly am I for? It feels bigger than it ought to, even to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of life, even when they’re not related to retirement?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It sort of moves around, if that makes sense. I’ll start with retirement, but then suddenly I’m thinking about whether my old patients are okay, or whether the finances of the handoff are solid, or whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd then it drifts into bigger things, like… what I’ll even be for once I’m fully retired. Which sounds dramatic when I say it. I know it’s probably out of proportion. But my mind keeps circling back to it anyway.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… yes, probably. Retirement is the big hook right now, so everything seems to hang on that, but I don’t think it’s only that.

My mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things—whether my old patients are really being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ve missed something I should’ve caught. And then it turns into that larger worry about whether I’ll be useless once I’m fully retired.

I know that sounds rather much. I mean, I had patients for years who would worry a toothache into something catastrophic before we even took an X-ray, and I’d tell them, “Let’s just look at what’s actually there.” Funny how hard that is to do for yourself. But yes, the worry feels out of proportion even to me.

It’s more that it started around when I began the formal retirement process—about a year and three months ago now.\n\nWhich is funny, because people keep saying retirement is supposed to be relaxing. I had this idea I’d spend more time painting, maybe organize the garage, finally sort through all these old dental journals I’ve kept for no good reason. But once the paperwork and planning started, that’s when I noticed all this really beginning.\n\nDoctor: Are most of your worries mainly about retirement?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, but it doesn’t stay neatly in that box. Retirement is sort of the doorway into it, if that makes sense.\n\nI worry about whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ve missed some detail in the transition. And then it turns into, you know, “What am I going to be once I’m not Dr. So-and-so at the clinic every day?” I’ll be rinsing out a watercolor brush or sorting mail and suddenly I’m thinking I’ll become useless, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo yes, retirement is a big part of it, but my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and even I can tell the size of the worry doesn’t always match the actual problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this worried on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely longer than six months. It’s been going on since I started the formal retirement process, which was about a year and three months ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the paperwork—forms, signatures, phone calls, all that business. I’ve never liked loose ends, even in the office I’d keep little notes everywhere. But then the worrying just sort of stayed with me, day after day, long after any one piece of it was handled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel a bit low, but then I’ll get distracted by something ridiculous like an old box of dental molds in the garage and spend an hour wondering why I kept them.\n\nSo… down, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d put that word on it. I’m more unsettled than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. More than I like to admit, probably.\n\nAt the clinic, I’ve had to slow down parts of the retirement handoff because I keep second-guessing decisions—whether I explained something clearly enough, whether I picked the right time to pass something over, whether some tiny detail is going to become a problem later. I’ll mean to handle one task and then lose half the morning going back over it.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. I used to paint to relax—watercolors, mostly, though I’m not especially good—and gardening was sort of my steady thing. Now even those feel harder to manage, like I can’t just settle into them the way I used to. So yes, it’s definitely getting in the way of daily life.\n\nDoctor: Aside from retirement, do you also find yourself worrying a lot about other areas of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not just the retirement paperwork, no. My mind sort of… circles around all sorts of things.\n\nI worry whether my old patients are being looked after properly, even though I know they’re capable hands. Then I’ll jump to whether the practice handoff is financially sound, or whether I missed something in the numbers. I was never really a “numbers man,” though dentistry has plenty of numbers—insurance codes, lab fees, all that nonsense.\n\nAnd then there’s this other thought that keeps coming up, which I don’t much like saying out loud: I worry I’ll become useless once I’m fully retired. Like once the office is gone, what exactly am I for? It feels bigger than it ought to, even to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of life, even when they’re not related to retirement?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It sort of moves around, if that makes sense. I’ll start with retirement, but then suddenly I’m thinking about whether my old patients are okay, or whether the finances of the handoff are solid, or whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd then it drifts into bigger things, like… what I’ll even be for once I’m fully retired. Which sounds dramatic when I say it. I know it’s probably out of proportion. But my mind keeps circling back to it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these worries would still be happening if you were not going through retirement?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Retirement is the big hook right now, so everything seems to hang on that, but I don’t think it’s only that.\n\nMy mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things—whether my old patients are really being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ve missed something I should’ve caught. And then it turns into that larger worry about whether I’ll be useless once I’m fully retired.\n\nI know that sounds rather much. I mean, I had patients for years who would worry a toothache into something catastrophic before we even took an X-ray, and I’d tell them, “Let’s just look at what’s actually there.” Funny how hard that is to do for yourself. But yes, the worry feels out of proportion even to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

And then it turns into, you know, “What am I going to be once I’m not Dr. So-and-so at the clinic every day?” I’ll be rinsing out a watercolor brush or sorting mail and suddenly I’m thinking I’ll become useless, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo yes, retirement is a big part of it, but my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and even I can tell the size of the worry doesn’t always match the actual problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this worried on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely longer than six months. It’s been going on since I started the formal retirement process, which was about a year and three months ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the paperwork—forms, signatures, phone calls, all that business. I’ve never liked loose ends, even in the office I’d keep little notes everywhere. But then the worrying just sort of stayed with me, day after day, long after any one piece of it was handled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel a bit low, but then I’ll get distracted by something ridiculous like an old box of dental molds in the garage and spend an hour wondering why I kept them.\n\nSo… down, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d put that word on it. I’m more unsettled than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. More than I like to admit, probably.\n\nAt the clinic, I’ve had to slow down parts of the retirement handoff because I keep second-guessing decisions—whether I explained something clearly enough, whether I picked the right time to pass something over, whether some tiny detail is going to become a problem later. I’ll mean to handle one task and then lose half the morning going back over it.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. I used to paint to relax—watercolors, mostly, though I’m not especially good—and gardening was sort of my steady thing. Now even those feel harder to manage, like I can’t just settle into them the way I used to. So yes, it’s definitely getting in the way of daily life.\n\nDoctor: Aside from retirement, do you also find yourself worrying a lot about other areas of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not just the retirement paperwork, no. My mind sort of… circles around all sorts of things.\n\nI worry whether my old patients are being looked after properly, even though I know they’re capable hands. Then I’ll jump to whether the practice handoff is financially sound, or whether I missed something in the numbers. I was never really a “numbers man,” though dentistry has plenty of numbers—insurance codes, lab fees, all that nonsense.\n\nAnd then there’s this other thought that keeps coming up, which I don’t much like saying out loud: I worry I’ll become useless once I’m fully retired. Like once the office is gone, what exactly am I for? It feels bigger than it ought to, even to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of life, even when they’re not related to retirement?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It sort of moves around, if that makes sense. I’ll start with retirement, but then suddenly I’m thinking about whether my old patients are okay, or whether the finances of the handoff are solid, or whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd then it drifts into bigger things, like… what I’ll even be for once I’m fully retired. Which sounds dramatic when I say it. I know it’s probably out of proportion. But my mind keeps circling back to it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these worries would still be happening if you were not going through retirement?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Retirement is the big hook right now, so everything seems to hang on that, but I don’t think it’s only that.\n\nMy mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things—whether my old patients are really being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ve missed something I should’ve caught. And then it turns into that larger worry about whether I’ll be useless once I’m fully retired.\n\nI know that sounds rather much. I mean, I had patients for years who would worry a toothache into something catastrophic before we even took an X-ray, and I’d tell them, “Let’s just look at what’s actually there.” Funny how hard that is to do for yourself. But yes, the worry feels out of proportion even to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tense or on edge a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. Tense… I suppose I’d have to think about what you mean by that. I don’t walk around clenching my fists or anything dramatic.\n\nI do catch myself sort of bracing, but then again, dentistry trains you into a certain posture—shoulders up, leaning over people, concentrating on tiny things while someone’s tongue is in the way. So sometimes I can’t tell what’s just habit and what’s… something else.\n\nBut “on edge a lot,” I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I’ve noticed the worrying more than anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

But once the paperwork and planning started, that’s when I noticed all this really beginning.\n\nDoctor: Are most of your worries mainly about retirement?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, but it doesn’t stay neatly in that box. Retirement is sort of the doorway into it, if that makes sense.\n\nI worry about whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ve missed some detail in the transition. And then it turns into, you know, “What am I going to be once I’m not Dr. So-and-so at the clinic every day?” I’ll be rinsing out a watercolor brush or sorting mail and suddenly I’m thinking I’ll become useless, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo yes, retirement is a big part of it, but my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and even I can tell the size of the worry doesn’t always match the actual problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this worried on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely longer than six months. It’s been going on since I started the formal retirement process, which was about a year and three months ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the paperwork—forms, signatures, phone calls, all that business. I’ve never liked loose ends, even in the office I’d keep little notes everywhere. But then the worrying just sort of stayed with me, day after day, long after any one piece of it was handled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel a bit low, but then I’ll get distracted by something ridiculous like an old box of dental molds in the garage and spend an hour wondering why I kept them.\n\nSo… down, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d put that word on it. I’m more unsettled than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. More than I like to admit, probably.\n\nAt the clinic, I’ve had to slow down parts of the retirement handoff because I keep second-guessing decisions—whether I explained something clearly enough, whether I picked the right time to pass something over, whether some tiny detail is going to become a problem later. I’ll mean to handle one task and then lose half the morning going back over it.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. I used to paint to relax—watercolors, mostly, though I’m not especially good—and gardening was sort of my steady thing. Now even those feel harder to manage, like I can’t just settle into them the way I used to. So yes, it’s definitely getting in the way of daily life.\n\nDoctor: Aside from retirement, do you also find yourself worrying a lot about other areas of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not just the retirement paperwork, no. My mind sort of… circles around all sorts of things.\n\nI worry whether my old patients are being looked after properly, even though I know they’re capable hands. Then I’ll jump to whether the practice handoff is financially sound, or whether I missed something in the numbers. I was never really a “numbers man,” though dentistry has plenty of numbers—insurance codes, lab fees, all that nonsense.\n\nAnd then there’s this other thought that keeps coming up, which I don’t much like saying out loud: I worry I’ll become useless once I’m fully retired. Like once the office is gone, what exactly am I for? It feels bigger than it ought to, even to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of life, even when they’re not related to retirement?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It sort of moves around, if that makes sense. I’ll start with retirement, but then suddenly I’m thinking about whether my old patients are okay, or whether the finances of the handoff are solid, or whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd then it drifts into bigger things, like… what I’ll even be for once I’m fully retired. Which sounds dramatic when I say it. I know it’s probably out of proportion. But my mind keeps circling back to it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these worries would still be happening if you were not going through retirement?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Retirement is the big hook right now, so everything seems to hang on that, but I don’t think it’s only that.\n\nMy mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things—whether my old patients are really being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ve missed something I should’ve caught. And then it turns into that larger worry about whether I’ll be useless once I’m fully retired.\n\nI know that sounds rather much. I mean, I had patients for years who would worry a toothache into something catastrophic before we even took an X-ray, and I’d tell them, “Let’s just look at what’s actually there.” Funny how hard that is to do for yourself. But yes, the worry feels out of proportion even to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tense or on edge a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. Tense… I suppose I’d have to think about what you mean by that. I don’t walk around clenching my fists or anything dramatic.\n\nI do catch myself sort of bracing, but then again, dentistry trains you into a certain posture—shoulders up, leaning over people, concentrating on tiny things while someone’s tongue is in the way. So sometimes I can’t tell what’s just habit and what’s… something else.\n\nBut “on edge a lot,” I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I’ve noticed the worrying more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired easily lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. By midafternoon I can feel quite worn out, not just “I’ve had a busy day” tired, but sort of… flattened.\n\nIt’s odd because I used to be on my feet at the practice for hours, and you’d think retirement would make me more rested. But even if I have a quiet morning, or I try to lie down for a bit, it doesn’t always seem to restore me. Then something simple like weeding the garden, or standing at the easel for a while, feels heavier than it ought to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Muscle tension… I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. My neck and shoulders have always been a bit of a weak spot, but after years bent over a dental chair, it’s hard to know what’s normal wear and tear and what’s something else.

But it doesn’t really stop there.\n\nOne concern just sort of hands off to the next. I’ll think, “Did I answer that email clearly?” and then I’m checking it again, and then I’m reviewing notes, and then maybe asking someone the same question I already asked because I can’t quite let it rest. It feels like I’m trying to be careful, but it gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems start after something particularly stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it one big stressful event, exactly. It’s more that it started around when I began the formal retirement process—about a year and three months ago now.\n\nWhich is funny, because people keep saying retirement is supposed to be relaxing. I had this idea I’d spend more time painting, maybe organize the garage, finally sort through all these old dental journals I’ve kept for no good reason. But once the paperwork and planning started, that’s when I noticed all this really beginning.\n\nDoctor: Are most of your worries mainly about retirement?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, but it doesn’t stay neatly in that box. Retirement is sort of the doorway into it, if that makes sense.\n\nI worry about whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ve missed some detail in the transition. And then it turns into, you know, “What am I going to be once I’m not Dr. So-and-so at the clinic every day?” I’ll be rinsing out a watercolor brush or sorting mail and suddenly I’m thinking I’ll become useless, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo yes, retirement is a big part of it, but my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and even I can tell the size of the worry doesn’t always match the actual problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this worried on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely longer than six months. It’s been going on since I started the formal retirement process, which was about a year and three months ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the paperwork—forms, signatures, phone calls, all that business. I’ve never liked loose ends, even in the office I’d keep little notes everywhere. But then the worrying just sort of stayed with me, day after day, long after any one piece of it was handled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel a bit low, but then I’ll get distracted by something ridiculous like an old box of dental molds in the garage and spend an hour wondering why I kept them.\n\nSo… down, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d put that word on it. I’m more unsettled than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. More than I like to admit, probably.\n\nAt the clinic, I’ve had to slow down parts of the retirement handoff because I keep second-guessing decisions—whether I explained something clearly enough, whether I picked the right time to pass something over, whether some tiny detail is going to become a problem later. I’ll mean to handle one task and then lose half the morning going back over it.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. I used to paint to relax—watercolors, mostly, though I’m not especially good—and gardening was sort of my steady thing. Now even those feel harder to manage, like I can’t just settle into them the way I used to. So yes, it’s definitely getting in the way of daily life.\n\nDoctor: Aside from retirement, do you also find yourself worrying a lot about other areas of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not just the retirement paperwork, no. My mind sort of… circles around all sorts of things.\n\nI worry whether my old patients are being looked after properly, even though I know they’re capable hands. Then I’ll jump to whether the practice handoff is financially sound, or whether I missed something in the numbers. I was never really a “numbers man,” though dentistry has plenty of numbers—insurance codes, lab fees, all that nonsense.\n\nAnd then there’s this other thought that keeps coming up, which I don’t much like saying out loud: I worry I’ll become useless once I’m fully retired. Like once the office is gone, what exactly am I for? It feels bigger than it ought to, even to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of life, even when they’re not related to retirement?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It sort of moves around, if that makes sense. I’ll start with retirement, but then suddenly I’m thinking about whether my old patients are okay, or whether the finances of the handoff are solid, or whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd then it drifts into bigger things, like… what I’ll even be for once I’m fully retired. Which sounds dramatic when I say it. I know it’s probably out of proportion. But my mind keeps circling back to it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these worries would still be happening if you were not going through retirement?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Retirement is the big hook right now, so everything seems to hang on that, but I don’t think it’s only that.\n\nMy mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things—whether my old patients are really being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ve missed something I should’ve caught. And then it turns into that larger worry about whether I’ll be useless once I’m fully retired.\n\nI know that sounds rather much. I mean, I had patients for years who would worry a toothache into something catastrophic before we even took an X-ray, and I’d tell them, “Let’s just look at what’s actually there.” Funny how hard that is to do for yourself. But yes, the worry feels out of proportion even to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tense or on edge a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. Tense… I suppose I’d have to think about what you mean by that. I don’t walk around clenching my fists or anything dramatic.\n\nI do catch myself sort of bracing, but then again, dentistry trains you into a certain posture—shoulders up, leaning over people, concentrating on tiny things while someone’s tongue is in the way. So sometimes I can’t tell what’s just habit and what’s… something else.\n\nBut “on edge a lot,” I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I’ve noticed the worrying more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired easily lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. By midafternoon I can feel quite worn out, not just “I’ve had a busy day” tired, but sort of… flattened.\n\nIt’s odd because I used to be on my feet at the practice for hours, and you’d think retirement would make me more rested. But even if I have a quiet morning, or I try to lie down for a bit, it doesn’t always seem to restore me. Then something simple like weeding the garden, or standing at the easel for a while, feels heavier than it ought to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having muscle tension lately?\n\nPatient: Muscle tension… I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. My neck and shoulders have always been a bit of a weak spot, but after years bent over a dental chair, it’s hard to know what’s normal wear and tear and what’s something else.\n\nI remember one hygienist I worked with used to say dentists all end up shaped like question marks by sixty, and she wasn’t entirely wrong. But lately? I can’t say I’ve noticed anything clearly different. Maybe some stiffness here and there, but I haven’t really been paying attention to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yes… sleep has been a bit of a nuisance lately. I’ll go to bed feeling tired enough, and then the house gets quiet—funny how everything seems louder then, even the refrigerator—and my mind starts going back over things.

Mostly practical things, I suppose. Patient transfers, bits of paperwork, money details, whether I’ve remembered this or that. When I had the practice, I used to do that sort of checking in the surgery office, and now it seems to follow me upstairs.

I do sleep some, but it doesn’t feel very settled. Quite a few mornings I’m awake at four or five, just lying there, and it’s as if I never properly dropped off in the first place.

Now even those feel harder to manage, like I can’t just settle into them the way I used to. I was never really a “numbers man,” though dentistry has plenty of numbers—insurance codes, lab fees, all that nonsense.\n\nAnd then there’s this other thought that keeps coming up, which I don’t much like saying out loud: I worry I’ll become useless once I’m fully retired. Like once the office is gone, what exactly am I for? It sort of moves around, if that makes sense. I’ll start with retirement, but then suddenly I’m thinking about whether my old patients are okay, or whether the finances of the handoff are solid, or whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd then it drifts into bigger things, like… what I’ll even be for once I’m fully retired. Which sounds dramatic when I say it. I know it’s probably out of proportion. But even if I have a quiet morning, or I try to lie down for a bit, it doesn’t always seem to restore me. Then something simple like weeding the garden, or standing at the easel for a while, feels heavier than it ought to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having muscle tension lately?\n\nPatient: Muscle tension… I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I’d like to think so, but… no, I don’t think it would all just disappear.

The retirement has become the main hook for it, I suppose, but my mind seems to keep circling around everything attached to it and then branching off. Whether the old patients are being looked after properly, whether the handoff was financially sensible, whether I’ve misjudged something… and then it turns into this larger question of what I’m meant to be if I’m not useful in that way anymore.

I don’t walk around clenching my fists or anything dramatic.\n\nI do catch myself sort of bracing, but then again, dentistry trains you into a certain posture—shoulders up, leaning over people, concentrating on tiny things while someone’s tongue is in the way. So sometimes I can’t tell what’s just habit and what’s… something else.\n\nBut “on edge a lot,” I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I’ve noticed the worrying more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired easily lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. By midafternoon I can feel quite worn out, not just “I’ve had a busy day” tired, but sort of… flattened.\n\nIt’s odd because I used to be on my feet at the practice for hours, and you’d think retirement would make me more rested. But even if I have a quiet morning, or I try to lie down for a bit, it doesn’t always seem to restore me. Then something simple like weeding the garden, or standing at the easel for a while, feels heavier than it ought to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having muscle tension lately?\n\nPatient: Muscle tension… I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. My neck and shoulders have always been a bit of a weak spot, but after years bent over a dental chair, it’s hard to know what’s normal wear and tear and what’s something else.\n\nI remember one hygienist I worked with used to say dentists all end up shaped like question marks by sixty, and she wasn’t entirely wrong. But lately? I can’t say I’ve noticed anything clearly different. Maybe some stiffness here and there, but I haven’t really been paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… sleep has been a bit of a nuisance lately. I’ll go to bed feeling tired enough, and then the house gets quiet—funny how everything seems louder then, even the refrigerator—and my mind starts going back over things.\n\nMostly practical things, I suppose. Patient transfers, bits of paperwork, money details, whether I’ve remembered this or that. When I had the practice, I used to do that sort of checking in the surgery office, and now it seems to follow me upstairs.\n\nI do sleep some, but it doesn’t feel very settled. Quite a few mornings I’m awake at four or five, just lying there, and it’s as if I never properly dropped off in the first place.\n\nDoctor: If the retirement process were completely resolved, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, but… no, I don’t think it would all just disappear.\n\nThe retirement has become the main hook for it, I suppose, but my mind seems to keep circling around everything attached to it and then branching off. Whether the old patients are being looked after properly, whether the handoff was financially sensible, whether I’ve misjudged something… and then it turns into this larger question of what I’m meant to be if I’m not useful in that way anymore.\n\nIt feels a bit excessive even as I’m doing it, which is the frustrating part. I’m not sure, to be honest. I wouldn’t say I’ve been snapping at people all over the place.

I do get a bit short when things are inefficient, but that’s always been true—ask anyone who’s worked with dental supply companies long enough. You order one very specific impression material and somehow three boxes of something else arrive, and then everyone looks surprised when you’re annoyed.

But lately… I don’t know. I haven’t especially noticed myself being more irritable than usual. Maybe my wife would have a better answer to that than I would.

And then it turns into, you know, “What am I going to be once I’m not Dr. So-and-so at the clinic every day?” I’ll be rinsing out a watercolor brush or sorting mail and suddenly I’m thinking I’ll become useless, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo yes, retirement is a big part of it, but my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and even I can tell the size of the worry doesn’t always match the actual problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this worried on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely longer than six months. It’s been going on since I started the formal retirement process, which was about a year and three months ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the paperwork—forms, signatures, phone calls, all that business. I’ve never liked loose ends, even in the office I’d keep little notes everywhere. But then the worrying just sort of stayed with me, day after day, long after any one piece of it was handled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel a bit low, but then I’ll get distracted by something ridiculous like an old box of dental molds in the garage and spend an hour wondering why I kept them.\n\nSo… down, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d put that word on it. I’m more unsettled than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. More than I like to admit, probably.\n\nAt the clinic, I’ve had to slow down parts of the retirement handoff because I keep second-guessing decisions—whether I explained something clearly enough, whether I picked the right time to pass something over, whether some tiny detail is going to become a problem later. I’ll mean to handle one task and then lose half the morning going back over it.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. I used to paint to relax—watercolors, mostly, though I’m not especially good—and gardening was sort of my steady thing. Now even those feel harder to manage, like I can’t just settle into them the way I used to. So yes, it’s definitely getting in the way of daily life.\n\nDoctor: Aside from retirement, do you also find yourself worrying a lot about other areas of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not just the retirement paperwork, no. My mind sort of… circles around all sorts of things.\n\nI worry whether my old patients are being looked after properly, even though I know they’re capable hands. Then I’ll jump to whether the practice handoff is financially sound, or whether I missed something in the numbers. I was never really a “numbers man,” though dentistry has plenty of numbers—insurance codes, lab fees, all that nonsense.\n\nAnd then there’s this other thought that keeps coming up, which I don’t much like saying out loud: I worry I’ll become useless once I’m fully retired. Like once the office is gone, what exactly am I for? It feels bigger than it ought to, even to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of life, even when they’re not related to retirement?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It sort of moves around, if that makes sense. I’ll start with retirement, but then suddenly I’m thinking about whether my old patients are okay, or whether the finances of the handoff are solid, or whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd then it drifts into bigger things, like… what I’ll even be for once I’m fully retired. Which sounds dramatic when I say it. I know it’s probably out of proportion. But my mind keeps circling back to it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these worries would still be happening if you were not going through retirement?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Retirement is the big hook right now, so everything seems to hang on that, but I don’t think it’s only that.\n\nMy mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things—whether my old patients are really being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ve missed something I should’ve caught. And then it turns into that larger worry about whether I’ll be useless once I’m fully retired.\n\nI know that sounds rather much. I mean, I had patients for years who would worry a toothache into something catastrophic before we even took an X-ray, and I’d tell them, “Let’s just look at what’s actually there.” Funny how hard that is to do for yourself. But yes, the worry feels out of proportion even to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tense or on edge a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. Tense… I suppose I’d have to think about what you mean by that. I don’t walk around clenching my fists or anything dramatic.\n\nI do catch myself sort of bracing, but then again, dentistry trains you into a certain posture—shoulders up, leaning over people, concentrating on tiny things while someone’s tongue is in the way. So sometimes I can’t tell what’s just habit and what’s… something else.\n\nBut “on edge a lot,” I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I’ve noticed the worrying more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired easily lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. By midafternoon I can feel quite worn out, not just “I’ve had a busy day” tired, but sort of… flattened.\n\nIt’s odd because I used to be on my feet at the practice for hours, and you’d think retirement would make me more rested. But even if I have a quiet morning, or I try to lie down for a bit, it doesn’t always seem to restore me. Then something simple like weeding the garden, or standing at the easel for a while, feels heavier than it ought to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having muscle tension lately?\n\nPatient: Muscle tension… I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. My neck and shoulders have always been a bit of a weak spot, but after years bent over a dental chair, it’s hard to know what’s normal wear and tear and what’s something else.\n\nI remember one hygienist I worked with used to say dentists all end up shaped like question marks by sixty, and she wasn’t entirely wrong. But lately? I can’t say I’ve noticed anything clearly different. Maybe some stiffness here and there, but I haven’t really been paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… sleep has been a bit of a nuisance lately. I’ll go to bed feeling tired enough, and then the house gets quiet—funny how everything seems louder then, even the refrigerator—and my mind starts going back over things.\n\nMostly practical things, I suppose. Patient transfers, bits of paperwork, money details, whether I’ve remembered this or that. When I had the practice, I used to do that sort of checking in the surgery office, and now it seems to follow me upstairs.\n\nI do sleep some, but it doesn’t feel very settled. Quite a few mornings I’m awake at four or five, just lying there, and it’s as if I never properly dropped off in the first place.\n\nDoctor: If the retirement process were completely resolved, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, but… no, I don’t think it would all just disappear.\n\nThe retirement has become the main hook for it, I suppose, but my mind seems to keep circling around everything attached to it and then branching off. Whether the old patients are being looked after properly, whether the handoff was financially sensible, whether I’ve misjudged something… and then it turns into this larger question of what I’m meant to be if I’m not useful in that way anymore.\n\nIt feels a bit excessive even as I’m doing it, which is the frustrating part. I can tell myself, “This is being handled,” and then ten minutes later I’m back at it again. So if the paperwork were magically finished tomorrow, I suspect I’d find something else nearby to chew over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, to be honest. I wouldn’t say I’ve been snapping at people all over the place.\n\nI do get a bit short when things are inefficient, but that’s always been true—ask anyone who’s worked with dental supply companies long enough. You order one very specific impression material and somehow three boxes of something else arrive, and then everyone looks surprised when you’re annoyed.\n\nBut lately… I don’t know. I haven’t especially noticed myself being more irritable than usual. Maybe my wife would have a better answer to that than I would.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Maybe my wife would have a better answer to that than I would.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling sad or empty much of the day nearly every day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 64 / dentist / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sad or empty… no, I’m not sure I’d put it that way.

I get flat sometimes, I suppose, but “sad most of the day nearly every day” sounds rather definite. My father used to say dentists are never cheerful in the way people expect anyway—you spend your life asking people questions while your hands are in their mouths, which doesn’t make for light conversation.

But no, I haven’t really noticed a steady sadness like that. It’s more that I get preoccupied and then the day sort of goes by.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.22, F32.9, F48.8, F06.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that my concentration’s gotten… unreliable. I’m a dentist, so I’m used to being precise, reading charts, keeping track of details. But lately I’ll be looking over a patient chart at the clinic and suddenly realize I’ve read the same line three times and couldn’t tell you what it said.\n\nIt happens outside work too. I paint watercolors—nothing professional, just something I picked up years ago—and I’ll be laying out a wash, trying to focus on the color or the paper, and then my mind just runs off to everything that could go wrong. And then I’m standing there with the brush in my hand thinking, “Where was I?” It’s frustrating. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things, in a way that feels hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I try to talk myself through it—almost like I would with a nervous patient in the chair, you know, calmly laying out what’s actually happening versus what I’m imagining. But it doesn’t really stop there.\n\nOne concern just sort of hands off to the next. I’ll think, “Did I answer that email clearly?” and then I’m checking it again, and then I’m reviewing notes, and then maybe asking someone the same question I already asked because I can’t quite let it rest. It feels like I’m trying to be careful, but it gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems start after something particularly stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it one big stressful event, exactly. It’s more that it started around when I began the formal retirement process—about a year and three months ago now.\n\nWhich is funny, because people keep saying retirement is supposed to be relaxing. I had this idea I’d spend more time painting, maybe organize the garage, finally sort through all these old dental journals I’ve kept for no good reason. But once the paperwork and planning started, that’s when I noticed all this really beginning.\n\nDoctor: Are most of your worries mainly about retirement?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, but it doesn’t stay neatly in that box. Retirement is sort of the doorway into it, if that makes sense.\n\nI worry about whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ve missed some detail in the transition. And then it turns into, you know, “What am I going to be once I’m not Dr. So-and-so at the clinic every day?” I’ll be rinsing out a watercolor brush or sorting mail and suddenly I’m thinking I’ll become useless, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo yes, retirement is a big part of it, but my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and even I can tell the size of the worry doesn’t always match the actual problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this worried on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely longer than six months. It’s been going on since I started the formal retirement process, which was about a year and three months ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the paperwork—forms, signatures, phone calls, all that business. I’ve never liked loose ends, even in the office I’d keep little notes everywhere. But then the worrying just sort of stayed with me, day after day, long after any one piece of it was handled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel a bit low, but then I’ll get distracted by something ridiculous like an old box of dental molds in the garage and spend an hour wondering why I kept them.\n\nSo… down, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d put that word on it. I’m more unsettled than sad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. More than I like to admit, probably.\n\nAt the clinic, I’ve had to slow down parts of the retirement handoff because I keep second-guessing decisions—whether I explained something clearly enough, whether I picked the right time to pass something over, whether some tiny detail is going to become a problem later. I’ll mean to handle one task and then lose half the morning going back over it.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. I used to paint to relax—watercolors, mostly, though I’m not especially good—and gardening was sort of my steady thing. Now even those feel harder to manage, like I can’t just settle into them the way I used to. So yes, it’s definitely getting in the way of daily life.\n\nDoctor: Aside from retirement, do you also find yourself worrying a lot about other areas of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s not just the retirement paperwork, no. My mind sort of… circles around all sorts of things.\n\nI worry whether my old patients are being looked after properly, even though I know they’re capable hands. Then I’ll jump to whether the practice handoff is financially sound, or whether I missed something in the numbers. I was never really a “numbers man,” though dentistry has plenty of numbers—insurance codes, lab fees, all that nonsense.\n\nAnd then there’s this other thought that keeps coming up, which I don’t much like saying out loud: I worry I’ll become useless once I’m fully retired. Like once the office is gone, what exactly am I for? It feels bigger than it ought to, even to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of life, even when they’re not related to retirement?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It sort of moves around, if that makes sense. I’ll start with retirement, but then suddenly I’m thinking about whether my old patients are okay, or whether the finances of the handoff are solid, or whether I missed something obvious.\n\nAnd then it drifts into bigger things, like… what I’ll even be for once I’m fully retired. Which sounds dramatic when I say it. I know it’s probably out of proportion. But my mind keeps circling back to it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these worries would still be happening if you were not going through retirement?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Retirement is the big hook right now, so everything seems to hang on that, but I don’t think it’s only that.\n\nMy mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things—whether my old patients are really being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ve missed something I should’ve caught. And then it turns into that larger worry about whether I’ll be useless once I’m fully retired.\n\nI know that sounds rather much. I mean, I had patients for years who would worry a toothache into something catastrophic before we even took an X-ray, and I’d tell them, “Let’s just look at what’s actually there.” Funny how hard that is to do for yourself. But yes, the worry feels out of proportion even to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tense or on edge a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. Tense… I suppose I’d have to think about what you mean by that. I don’t walk around clenching my fists or anything dramatic.\n\nI do catch myself sort of bracing, but then again, dentistry trains you into a certain posture—shoulders up, leaning over people, concentrating on tiny things while someone’s tongue is in the way. So sometimes I can’t tell what’s just habit and what’s… something else.\n\nBut “on edge a lot,” I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I’ve noticed the worrying more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired easily lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. By midafternoon I can feel quite worn out, not just “I’ve had a busy day” tired, but sort of… flattened.\n\nIt’s odd because I used to be on my feet at the practice for hours, and you’d think retirement would make me more rested. But even if I have a quiet morning, or I try to lie down for a bit, it doesn’t always seem to restore me. Then something simple like weeding the garden, or standing at the easel for a while, feels heavier than it ought to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having muscle tension lately?\n\nPatient: Muscle tension… I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. My neck and shoulders have always been a bit of a weak spot, but after years bent over a dental chair, it’s hard to know what’s normal wear and tear and what’s something else.\n\nI remember one hygienist I worked with used to say dentists all end up shaped like question marks by sixty, and she wasn’t entirely wrong. But lately? I can’t say I’ve noticed anything clearly different. Maybe some stiffness here and there, but I haven’t really been paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… sleep has been a bit of a nuisance lately. I’ll go to bed feeling tired enough, and then the house gets quiet—funny how everything seems louder then, even the refrigerator—and my mind starts going back over things.\n\nMostly practical things, I suppose. Patient transfers, bits of paperwork, money details, whether I’ve remembered this or that. When I had the practice, I used to do that sort of checking in the surgery office, and now it seems to follow me upstairs.\n\nI do sleep some, but it doesn’t feel very settled. Quite a few mornings I’m awake at four or five, just lying there, and it’s as if I never properly dropped off in the first place.\n\nDoctor: If the retirement process were completely resolved, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, but… no, I don’t think it would all just disappear.\n\nThe retirement has become the main hook for it, I suppose, but my mind seems to keep circling around everything attached to it and then branching off. Whether the old patients are being looked after properly, whether the handoff was financially sensible, whether I’ve misjudged something… and then it turns into this larger question of what I’m meant to be if I’m not useful in that way anymore.\n\nIt feels a bit excessive even as I’m doing it, which is the frustrating part. I can tell myself, “This is being handled,” and then ten minutes later I’m back at it again. So if the paperwork were magically finished tomorrow, I suspect I’d find something else nearby to chew over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, to be honest. I wouldn’t say I’ve been snapping at people all over the place.\n\nI do get a bit short when things are inefficient, but that’s always been true—ask anyone who’s worked with dental supply companies long enough. You order one very specific impression material and somehow three boxes of something else arrive, and then everyone looks surprised when you’re annoyed.\n\nBut lately… I don’t know. I haven’t especially noticed myself being more irritable than usual. Maybe my wife would have a better answer to that than I would.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty much of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty… no, I’m not sure I’d put it that way.\n\nI get flat sometimes, I suppose, but “sad most of the day nearly every day” sounds rather definite. My father used to say dentists are never cheerful in the way people expect anyway—you spend your life asking people questions while your hands are in their mouths, which doesn’t make for light conversation.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed a steady sadness like that. It’s more that I get preoccupied and then the day sort of goes by.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
